Like maybe then I wouldn’t be constantly thinking about it when I’m at the warehouse, wondering if people are noticing while I’m working or walking past them.\n\nAnd at salsa night, it’s even worse, because people are right there in front of me, looking at my face, and I’m thinking, “They can see it. They can tell something is off.” So part of me does think, if that side looked different, then maybe I could relax and not feel like everyone is noticing it.\n\nBut it’s not like I have some clear plan or anything. It’s more that I can’t stop thinking about that one side looking messed up. Even when people say they don’t see anything, I still feel like they must notice it and are just not saying it.\n\nDoctor: Has your concern about the left side of your face affected your ability to do your job at the warehouse or enjoy activities like salsa night?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it has, more than I really want to admit. At the warehouse, I’ve actually been written up because I get behind. It’s not that I don’t know how to do the job, it’s more like I’ll get stuck worrying about how I’m standing or which side of my face people can see, and then I slow down or avoid going past certain people, or I’ll take longer than I should because I’m trying not to be noticed. Then the work piles up, and my supervisor sees that I’m delayed.\n\nAnd with salsa night, yeah, I’ve started skipping it. I used to like going, but now it feels like everybody is close up, looking right at me, and I can’t enjoy the dancing because I’m thinking about my face the whole time. I’ve also been avoiding soccer and family gatherings sometimes, especially if I think people might take pictures. That’s a big one. If someone pulls out a phone, I just feel like I need to get out of there.\n\nSo, yeah, it’s affecting work and the things I used to do for fun. It’s like I’m planning my whole day around not having people look at me too much.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would be satisfied with how you look if the left side of your face was 'fixed', or would you then find something else about your appearance to worry about?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I want to say I’d be satisfied, because in my head it feels like that’s the whole problem. Like, I’m here because I can’t stop thinking about the left side of my face looking crooked and messed up. That’s the thing I keep coming back to over and over.\n\nIf that part looked right, I imagine I’d be able to go to the warehouse and not constantly feel like people are noticing it when I’m walking by or talking to them. Or at salsa night, maybe I could actually just dance instead of thinking, “They’re seeing that side of my face, they can tell something’s wrong,” even when people say they don’t see anything.\n\nSo… I guess I hope fixing that would make it stop. But I don’t totally know. Right now it’s so focused on that left side that I can’t really picture anything else mattering as much. It just feels like if that one thing wasn’t there, I could finally relax.\n\nDoctor: Is it only how you look that bothers you, or are there other things about yourself that you worry about?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly how I look, yeah. I mean… I guess everybody has things they worry about, but the thing that takes over for me is my face. Specifically the left side. That’s what I can’t get away from.\n\nLike, I’m here because I can’t stop thinking it looks crooked or messed up somehow, and then I start feeling like everyone can see it. At the warehouse, even if I’m just stocking or walking past somebody, I’m thinking about what angle they’re seeing me from, whether they’re noticing that side of my face. And at salsa night it’s worse because people are right there, facing me, and I feel like they must be looking at it, even when they say they don’t see anything.\n\nSo I wouldn’t say I’m sitting around worrying about, like, my whole personality or every part of my life. It’s really this appearance thing, this one part of my face, and it just keeps looping in my head.\n\nDoctor: Has your worry about the left side of your face caused you to avoid social interactions or relationships altogether?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t think I’ve cut off everyone completely, but I definitely avoid a lot now. Like, if I know there’s going to be people around where I can’t control how they see me, or if there might be pictures, I’ll just not go.\n\nI’ve started skipping soccer, even though I used to like it, because after the game people hang around and talk, and I start thinking they’re looking at the left side of my face. Salsa nights too… I’ve skipped a bunch of those because it feels too close, like people are right in front of me and there’s nowhere to hide. And family gatherings have gotten harder, because somebody always wants to take pictures or videos, and then I’m stuck worrying about how my face looks in them.\n\nEven at work it affects me socially, kind of. At the warehouse I’ve been written up because I get delayed, and part of that is me trying to avoid walking by people or being seen from certain angles. So, yeah… I wouldn’t say I have no relationships at all, but I’m definitely pulling away from people and avoiding things so I don’t have to deal with them looking at me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think people would judge you or think less of you as a person if they saw the left side of your face?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that’s what I’m afraid of. Like, I know people say they don’t see anything, or they’ll tell me it looks normal, but it doesn’t feel normal to me. When I’m at the warehouse and someone walks past me on that side, I immediately start thinking, “They saw it. They noticed my face is crooked. Now they’re thinking something’s wrong with me.”\n\nAnd it’s not even always like they say anything. It’s more that I imagine what they’re thinking. Like maybe they think I look weird, or off, or unattractive, or like I don’t take care of myself or something. I know that probably sounds like a lot to put on one side of my face, but in the moment it feels really real. If someone looks at me for even a second too long, especially from the left, I start feeling like they’re judging me.\n\nAt salsa night it’s even worse because you’re close to people, face to face, and I can’t stop thinking they’re noticing that side. Even if they’re just focused on dancing, in my head I’m thinking, “They can tell. They’re being polite, but they see it.” So yeah, I guess I do worry they’d think less of me, not because they actually said that, but because I can’t stop thinking my face looks messed up and that everyone can see it.\n\nDoctor: Has your concern about the left side of your face caused you significant distress or problems in your daily life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… definitely. It’s not just like I look in the mirror and feel bad for a minute and then go on with my day. It actually messes with things I’m supposed to be doing.\n\nAt work, at the warehouse, I’ve been written up because I get delayed. Like, I’ll take longer than I should because I’m trying to avoid certain aisles or avoid walking past people from the left side, or I’ll kind of pause and wait until someone moves so they don’t see me from that angle. And I know from the outside it probably just looks like I’m not doing my job fast enough, but in my head I’m trying to manage how people see my face the whole time. It’s exhausting, and then I get in trouble for being behind.\n\nAnd outside of work, it’s made my life smaller, I guess. I’ve been skipping soccer, and I used to really enjoy that. But then after the game people stand around talking, and I start worrying they’re looking at me. Salsa nights too — I’ve skipped those because it feels too close, like people are right in front of my face and I can’t hide the left side. And family stuff… I’ve been avoiding some gatherings because somebody always wants pictures or videos, and then I’m stuck thinking about how bad I’m going to look or who’s going to see it later.\n\nSo yeah, it causes a lot of distress. It’s affecting work, and it’s affecting me seeing people, even people I actually want to see.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still worry about people judging you if the left side of your face looked perfectly normal?\n\nPatient: I mean… if it actually looked perfectly normal to me, then maybe I wouldn’t worry the same way. But that’s the problem — it doesn’t look normal to me. I can’t stop seeing the left side as crooked or messed up, and once I see it that way, I just assume other people can see it too.\n\nLike at the warehouse, even if nobody says anything, I’m thinking they noticed it when they walked past me. Or at salsa night, when someone is close to my face, I’m not really able to believe they don’t see what I see. Even when people tell me they don’t notice anything, it doesn’t really calm me down for long.\n\nSo… I guess if I truly believed it looked perfectly normal, maybe I wouldn’t be as scared of being judged. But I’m here because I can’t get myself to believe that. I keep thinking something is wrong with that side of my face, and then I start thinking everyone else is noticing it too.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."

Your goal is to answer the doctor's question naturally, using the\ninformation you are given.\n\nAnswer with the [Targeted Info], told in the way [Conversation Style] describes.\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Do you think people would judge you or think less of you as a person if they saw the left side of your face?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that’s what I’m afraid of. Like, I know people say they don’t see anything, or they’ll tell me it looks normal, but it doesn’t feel normal to me. When I’m at the warehouse and someone walks past me on that side, I immediately start thinking, “They saw it. They noticed my face is crooked. Now they’re thinking something’s wrong with me.”\n\nAnd it’s not even always like they say anything. It’s more that I imagine what they’re thinking. Like maybe they think I look weird, or off, or unattractive, or like I don’t take care of myself or something. I know that probably sounds like a lot to put on one side of my face, but in the moment it feels really real. If someone looks at me for even a second too long, especially from the left, I start feeling like they’re judging me.\n\nAt salsa night it’s even worse because you’re close to people, face to face, and I can’t stop thinking they’re noticing that side. Even if they’re just focused on dancing, in my head I’m thinking, “They can tell. They’re being polite, but they see it.” So yeah, I guess I do worry they’d think less of me, not because they actually said that, but because I can’t stop thinking my face looks messed up and that everyone can see it.\n\nDoctor: Has your concern about the left side of your face caused you significant distress or problems in your daily life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… definitely. It’s not just like I look in the mirror and feel bad for a minute and then go on with my day. It actually messes with things I’m supposed to be doing.\n\nAt work, at the warehouse, I’ve been written up because I get delayed. Like, I’ll take longer than I should because I’m trying to avoid certain aisles or avoid walking past people from the left side, or I’ll kind of pause and wait until someone moves so they don’t see me from that angle. And I know from the outside it probably just looks like I’m not doing my job fast enough, but in my head I’m trying to manage how people see my face the whole time. It’s exhausting, and then I get in trouble for being behind.\n\nAnd outside of work, it’s made my life smaller, I guess. I’ve been skipping soccer, and I used to really enjoy that. But then after the game people stand around talking, and I start worrying they’re looking at me. Salsa nights too — I’ve skipped those because it feels too close, like people are right in front of my face and I can’t hide the left side. And family stuff… I’ve been avoiding some gatherings because somebody always wants pictures or videos, and then I’m stuck thinking about how bad I’m going to look or who’s going to see it later.\n\nSo yeah, it causes a lot of distress. It’s affecting work, and it’s affecting me seeing people, even people I actually want to see.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still worry about people judging you if the left side of your face looked perfectly normal?\n\nPatient: I mean… if it actually looked perfectly normal to me, then maybe I wouldn’t worry the same way. But that’s the problem — it doesn’t look normal to me. I can’t stop seeing the left side as crooked or messed up, and once I see it that way, I just assume other people can see it too.\n\nLike at the warehouse, even if nobody says anything, I’m thinking they noticed it when they walked past me. Or at salsa night, when someone is close to my face, I’m not really able to believe they don’t see what I see. Even when people tell me they don’t notice anything, it doesn’t really calm me down for long.\n\nSo… I guess if I truly believed it looked perfectly normal, maybe I wouldn’t be as scared of being judged. But I’m here because I can’t get myself to believe that. I keep thinking something is wrong with that side of my face, and then I start thinking everyone else is noticing it too.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you think you would worry about people judging you based on other aspects of your appearance if the left side of your face looked perfectly normal to you?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 30 / stocker_or_order_filler / high_school\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Preoccupation_with_Appearance\nmanifestation: \"I’m here because I can’t stop thinking that the left side of my face looks crooked and messed up, like everyone at the warehouse or at salsa night is noticing it even when they tell me they don’t see anything.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t think so… not in the same way. I mean, I can have normal stuff where I don’t love how I look, like anyone, but this isn’t really about my whole appearance. It keeps coming back to the left side of my face.

Like, if that side looked normal to me — really normal, not just people saying it’s normal — I think most of the judging fear would probably go down a lot. Because what I’m stuck on is that it looks crooked or messed up, and then I start imagining everyone else seeing that too. At the warehouse, if someone passes me on that side, that’s what I’m thinking about. At salsa night, when someone is close to me, I’m thinking they’re noticing that side of my face.

So no, I don’t think I’d just switch to worrying about, like, my hair or my clothes or something else. I’m here because I can’t stop thinking about that one thing — the left side of my face — even when people tell me they don’t see anything.

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n12\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF45.2, F42.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I’m here because I just… I can’t get my mind off my face. Specifically the left side. It feels like it looks crooked, like something is off or messed up, and I know people say they don’t see what I’m talking about, but I can’t really believe them.\n\nIt’s gotten to where at work, at the warehouse, I’m thinking about whether people are noticing it while I’m stocking or moving stuff around. And then if I go out, like to salsa night, it’s the same thing but worse because people are looking at you more, you know? I keep feeling like they’re seeing the left side of my face and thinking it looks strange, even if nobody says anything. Even when someone tells me, “No, you look fine,” I still feel like they’re just being nice or they don’t want to say it.\n\nSo… yeah, that’s mainly why I came in. I’m kind of stuck on it all the time.\n\nDoctor: Is it specifically the idea that the left side of your face is crooked that bothers you, or is it more about feeling like you're unattractive in general?\n\nPatient: It’s more specifically the left side of my face, yeah. I mean, I don’t feel great about how I look in general because of it, but the thing I get stuck on is that it looks crooked or messed up on that side. Like if someone is standing on my left or I’m turned a certain way, I start thinking, “They can see it, they’re noticing how off it looks.”\n\nAt the warehouse it happens a lot because I’m around people all day, moving past them, turning my head, talking while stocking stuff, and I’ll be thinking about whether they’re looking at that side of my face. And at salsa night it’s even harder because people are face-to-face with you and you’re moving close to them, so I feel like there’s no way they’re not seeing it.\n\nPeople tell me they don’t notice anything, but it doesn’t really settle it for me. I keep thinking maybe they’re just being polite or they don’t want to make me feel bad. So, yeah, it’s not just a general “I’m unattractive” thing. It’s mainly that one part of my face looks wrong to me, and then everything else kind of spirals from that.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever think that maybe your face actually doesn't look crooked, and it's just your imagination?\n\nPatient: I mean… I try to tell myself that sometimes. Like, I’ll think, “Maybe I’m making too much of it, maybe other people really don’t see it.” But it doesn’t stick. It’s like the thought comes right back that the left side of my face looks crooked or messed up.\n\nEspecially at the warehouse, when I’m around people and moving past them or talking to someone, I’ll suddenly be thinking, “They’re looking at that side, they can tell.” And then at salsa night it’s even worse because people are close up, face-to-face, and I feel like there’s no way they’re not noticing it.\n\nSo yeah, part of me hears people when they say they don’t see anything, but I can’t really believe it. I keep thinking they’re just being nice, or maybe they do notice but don’t want to say it. It feels too real to just be my imagination, even though I wish I could convince myself it was.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever thought that maybe your face could look okay, or even good, if you could just change something about the left side, like with makeup or surgery?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, that’s kind of the thing I get stuck on, I guess. I keep thinking if the left side of my face could just be… fixed somehow, or not look so crooked, then maybe I’d feel okay. Like maybe then I wouldn’t be constantly thinking about it when I’m at the warehouse, wondering if people are noticing while I’m working or walking past them.\n\nAnd at salsa night, it’s even worse, because people are right there in front of me, looking at my face, and I’m thinking, “They can see it. They can tell something is off.” So part of me does think, if that side looked different, then maybe I could relax and not feel like everyone is noticing it.\n\nBut it’s not like I have some clear plan or anything. It’s more that I can’t stop thinking about that one side looking messed up. Even when people say they don’t see anything, I still feel like they must notice it and are just not saying it.\n\nDoctor: Has your concern about the left side of your face affected your ability to do your job at the warehouse or enjoy activities like salsa night?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it has, more than I really want to admit. At the warehouse, I’ve actually been written up because I get behind. It’s not that I don’t know how to do the job, it’s more like I’ll get stuck worrying about how I’m standing or which side of my face people can see, and then I slow down or avoid going past certain people, or I’ll take longer than I should because I’m trying not to be noticed. Then the work piles up, and my supervisor sees that I’m delayed.\n\nAnd with salsa night, yeah, I’ve started skipping it. I used to like going, but now it feels like everybody is close up, looking right at me, and I can’t enjoy the dancing because I’m thinking about my face the whole time. I’ve also been avoiding soccer and family gatherings sometimes, especially if I think people might take pictures. That’s a big one. If someone pulls out a phone, I just feel like I need to get out of there.\n\nSo, yeah, it’s affecting work and the things I used to do for fun. It’s like I’m planning my whole day around not having people look at me too much.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would be satisfied with how you look if the left side of your face was 'fixed', or would you then find something else about your appearance to worry about?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I want to say I’d be satisfied, because in my head it feels like that’s the whole problem. Like, I’m here because I can’t stop thinking about the left side of my face looking crooked and messed up. That’s the thing I keep coming back to over and over.\n\nIf that part looked right, I imagine I’d be able to go to the warehouse and not constantly feel like people are noticing it when I’m walking by or talking to them. Or at salsa night, maybe I could actually just dance instead of thinking, “They’re seeing that side of my face, they can tell something’s wrong,” even when people say they don’t see anything.\n\nSo… I guess I hope fixing that would make it stop. But I don’t totally know. Right now it’s so focused on that left side that I can’t really picture anything else mattering as much. It just feels like if that one thing wasn’t there, I could finally relax.\n\nDoctor: Is it only how you look that bothers you, or are there other things about yourself that you worry about?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly how I look, yeah. I mean… I guess everybody has things they worry about, but the thing that takes over for me is my face. Specifically the left side. That’s what I can’t get away from.\n\nLike, I’m here because I can’t stop thinking it looks crooked or messed up somehow, and then I start feeling like everyone can see it. At the warehouse, even if I’m just stocking or walking past somebody, I’m thinking about what angle they’re seeing me from, whether they’re noticing that side of my face. And at salsa night it’s worse because people are right there, facing me, and I feel like they must be looking at it, even when they say they don’t see anything.\n\nSo I wouldn’t say I’m sitting around worrying about, like, my whole personality or every part of my life. It’s really this appearance thing, this one part of my face, and it just keeps looping in my head.\n\nDoctor: Has your worry about the left side of your face caused you to avoid social interactions or relationships altogether?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t think I’ve cut off everyone completely, but I definitely avoid a lot now. Like, if I know there’s going to be people around where I can’t control how they see me, or if there might be pictures, I’ll just not go.\n\nI’ve started skipping soccer, even though I used to like it, because after the game people hang around and talk, and I start thinking they’re looking at the left side of my face. Salsa nights too… I’ve skipped a bunch of those because it feels too close, like people are right in front of me and there’s nowhere to hide. And family gatherings have gotten harder, because somebody always wants to take pictures or videos, and then I’m stuck worrying about how my face looks in them.\n\nEven at work it affects me socially, kind of. At the warehouse I’ve been written up because I get delayed, and part of that is me trying to avoid walking by people or being seen from certain angles. So, yeah… I wouldn’t say I have no relationships at all, but I’m definitely pulling away from people and avoiding things so I don’t have to deal with them looking at me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think people would judge you or think less of you as a person if they saw the left side of your face?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that’s what I’m afraid of. Like, I know people say they don’t see anything, or they’ll tell me it looks normal, but it doesn’t feel normal to me. When I’m at the warehouse and someone walks past me on that side, I immediately start thinking, “They saw it. They noticed my face is crooked. Now they’re thinking something’s wrong with me.”\n\nAnd it’s not even always like they say anything. It’s more that I imagine what they’re thinking. Like maybe they think I look weird, or off, or unattractive, or like I don’t take care of myself or something. I know that probably sounds like a lot to put on one side of my face, but in the moment it feels really real. If someone looks at me for even a second too long, especially from the left, I start feeling like they’re judging me.\n\nAt salsa night it’s even worse because you’re close to people, face to face, and I can’t stop thinking they’re noticing that side. Even if they’re just focused on dancing, in my head I’m thinking, “They can tell. They’re being polite, but they see it.” So yeah, I guess I do worry they’d think less of me, not because they actually said that, but because I can’t stop thinking my face looks messed up and that everyone can see it.\n\nDoctor: Has your concern about the left side of your face caused you significant distress or problems in your daily life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… definitely. It’s not just like I look in the mirror and feel bad for a minute and then go on with my day. It actually messes with things I’m supposed to be doing.\n\nAt work, at the warehouse, I’ve been written up because I get delayed. Like, I’ll take longer than I should because I’m trying to avoid certain aisles or avoid walking past people from the left side, or I’ll kind of pause and wait until someone moves so they don’t see me from that angle. And I know from the outside it probably just looks like I’m not doing my job fast enough, but in my head I’m trying to manage how people see my face the whole time. It’s exhausting, and then I get in trouble for being behind.\n\nAnd outside of work, it’s made my life smaller, I guess. I’ve been skipping soccer, and I used to really enjoy that. But then after the game people stand around talking, and I start worrying they’re looking at me. Salsa nights too — I’ve skipped those because it feels too close, like people are right in front of my face and I can’t hide the left side. And family stuff… I’ve been avoiding some gatherings because somebody always wants pictures or videos, and then I’m stuck thinking about how bad I’m going to look or who’s going to see it later.\n\nSo yeah, it causes a lot of distress. It’s affecting work, and it’s affecting me seeing people, even people I actually want to see.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still worry about people judging you if the left side of your face looked perfectly normal?\n\nPatient: I mean… if it actually looked perfectly normal to me, then maybe I wouldn’t worry the same way. But that’s the problem — it doesn’t look normal to me. I can’t stop seeing the left side as crooked or messed up, and once I see it that way, I just assume other people can see it too.\n\nLike at the warehouse, even if nobody says anything, I’m thinking they noticed it when they walked past me. Or at salsa night, when someone is close to my face, I’m not really able to believe they don’t see what I see. Even when people tell me they don’t notice anything, it doesn’t really calm me down for long.\n\nSo… I guess if I truly believed it looked perfectly normal, maybe I wouldn’t be as scared of being judged. But I’m here because I can’t get myself to believe that. I keep thinking something is wrong with that side of my face, and then I start thinking everyone else is noticing it too.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would worry about people judging you based on other aspects of your appearance if the left side of your face looked perfectly normal to you?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the same way. I mean, I can have normal stuff where I don’t love how I look, like anyone, but this isn’t really about my whole appearance. It keeps coming back to the left side of my face.\n\nLike, if that side looked normal to me — really normal, not just people saying it’s normal — I think most of the judging fear would probably go down a lot. Because what I’m stuck on is that it looks crooked or messed up, and then I start imagining everyone else seeing that too. At the warehouse, if someone passes me on that side, that’s what I’m thinking about. At salsa night, when someone is close to me, I’m thinking they’re noticing that side of my face.\n\nSo no, I don’t think I’d just switch to worrying about, like, my hair or my clothes or something else. I’m here because I can’t stop thinking about that one thing — the left side of my face — even when people tell me they don’t see anything.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

Or at salsa night, maybe I could actually just dance instead of thinking, “They’re seeing that side of my face, they can tell something’s wrong,” even when people say they don’t see anything.\n\nSo… I guess I hope fixing that would make it stop. But I don’t totally know. Right now it’s so focused on that left side that I can’t really picture anything else mattering as much. It just feels like if that one thing wasn’t there, I could finally relax.\n\nDoctor: Is it only how you look that bothers you, or are there other things about yourself that you worry about?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly how I look, yeah. I mean… I guess everybody has things they worry about, but the thing that takes over for me is my face. Specifically the left side. That’s what I can’t get away from.\n\nLike, I’m here because I can’t stop thinking it looks crooked or messed up somehow, and then I start feeling like everyone can see it. At the warehouse, even if I’m just stocking or walking past somebody, I’m thinking about what angle they’re seeing me from, whether they’re noticing that side of my face. And at salsa night it’s worse because people are right there, facing me, and I feel like they must be looking at it, even when they say they don’t see anything.\n\nSo I wouldn’t say I’m sitting around worrying about, like, my whole personality or every part of my life. It’s really this appearance thing, this one part of my face, and it just keeps looping in my head.\n\nDoctor: Has your worry about the left side of your face caused you to avoid social interactions or relationships altogether?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t think I’ve cut off everyone completely, but I definitely avoid a lot now. Like, if I know there’s going to be people around where I can’t control how they see me, or if there might be pictures, I’ll just not go.\n\nI’ve started skipping soccer, even though I used to like it, because after the game people hang around and talk, and I start thinking they’re looking at the left side of my face. Salsa nights too… I’ve skipped a bunch of those because it feels too close, like people are right in front of me and there’s nowhere to hide. And family gatherings have gotten harder, because somebody always wants to take pictures or videos, and then I’m stuck worrying about how my face looks in them.\n\nEven at work it affects me socially, kind of. At the warehouse I’ve been written up because I get delayed, and part of that is me trying to avoid walking by people or being seen from certain angles. So, yeah… I wouldn’t say I have no relationships at all, but I’m definitely pulling away from people and avoiding things so I don’t have to deal with them looking at me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think people would judge you or think less of you as a person if they saw the left side of your face?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that’s what I’m afraid of. Like, I know people say they don’t see anything, or they’ll tell me it looks normal, but it doesn’t feel normal to me. When I’m at the warehouse and someone walks past me on that side, I immediately start thinking, “They saw it. They noticed my face is crooked. Now they’re thinking something’s wrong with me.”\n\nAnd it’s not even always like they say anything. It’s more that I imagine what they’re thinking. Like maybe they think I look weird, or off, or unattractive, or like I don’t take care of myself or something. I know that probably sounds like a lot to put on one side of my face, but in the moment it feels really real. If someone looks at me for even a second too long, especially from the left, I start feeling like they’re judging me.\n\nAt salsa night it’s even worse because you’re close to people, face to face, and I can’t stop thinking they’re noticing that side. Even if they’re just focused on dancing, in my head I’m thinking, “They can tell. They’re being polite, but they see it.” So yeah, I guess I do worry they’d think less of me, not because they actually said that, but because I can’t stop thinking my face looks messed up and that everyone can see it.\n\nDoctor: Has your concern about the left side of your face caused you significant distress or problems in your daily life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… definitely. It’s not just like I look in the mirror and feel bad for a minute and then go on with my day. It actually messes with things I’m supposed to be doing.\n\nAt work, at the warehouse, I’ve been written up because I get delayed. Like, I’ll take longer than I should because I’m trying to avoid certain aisles or avoid walking past people from the left side, or I’ll kind of pause and wait until someone moves so they don’t see me from that angle. And I know from the outside it probably just looks like I’m not doing my job fast enough, but in my head I’m trying to manage how people see my face the whole time. It’s exhausting, and then I get in trouble for being behind.\n\nAnd outside of work, it’s made my life smaller, I guess. I’ve been skipping soccer, and I used to really enjoy that. But then after the game people stand around talking, and I start worrying they’re looking at me. Salsa nights too — I’ve skipped those because it feels too close, like people are right in front of my face and I can’t hide the left side. And family stuff… I’ve been avoiding some gatherings because somebody always wants pictures or videos, and then I’m stuck thinking about how bad I’m going to look or who’s going to see it later.\n\nSo yeah, it causes a lot of distress. It’s affecting work, and it’s affecting me seeing people, even people I actually want to see.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still worry about people judging you if the left side of your face looked perfectly normal?\n\nPatient: I mean… if it actually looked perfectly normal to me, then maybe I wouldn’t worry the same way. But that’s the problem — it doesn’t look normal to me. I can’t stop seeing the left side as crooked or messed up, and once I see it that way, I just assume other people can see it too.\n\nLike at the warehouse, even if nobody says anything, I’m thinking they noticed it when they walked past me. Or at salsa night, when someone is close to my face, I’m not really able to believe they don’t see what I see. Even when people tell me they don’t notice anything, it doesn’t really calm me down for long.\n\nSo… I guess if I truly believed it looked perfectly normal, maybe I wouldn’t be as scared of being judged. But I’m here because I can’t get myself to believe that. I keep thinking something is wrong with that side of my face, and then I start thinking everyone else is noticing it too.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would worry about people judging you based on other aspects of your appearance if the left side of your face looked perfectly normal to you?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the same way. I mean, I can have normal stuff where I don’t love how I look, like anyone, but this isn’t really about my whole appearance. It keeps coming back to the left side of my face.\n\nLike, if that side looked normal to me — really normal, not just people saying it’s normal — I think most of the judging fear would probably go down a lot. Because what I’m stuck on is that it looks crooked or messed up, and then I start imagining everyone else seeing that too. At the warehouse, if someone passes me on that side, that’s what I’m thinking about. At salsa night, when someone is close to me, I’m thinking they’re noticing that side of my face.\n\nSo no, I don’t think I’d just switch to worrying about, like, my hair or my clothes or something else. Not really… I mean, I can pick at normal things sometimes, like if my hair looks bad or I’m tired or whatever, but that’s not what gets stuck in my head.

It’s really the left side of my face. That’s the thing I can’t stop thinking about. I keep feeling like it looks crooked, or uneven, or just messed up somehow, and then once I notice it, I can’t unsee it. And then I start assuming everyone else can see it too — like people at the warehouse, if they’re walking past me or standing on that side, I’m thinking, “They noticed. They’re looking at it.” Same thing at salsa night, because people are close up and facing me, and I feel like there’s nowhere to hide it.

Even when someone says they don’t see anything, I don’t really believe it for very long. So no, it’s not like I’m worried about every part of how I look. It’s mostly that one thing, the left side of my face, and it just takes over.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I’m here because I just… I can’t get my mind off my face. Specifically the left side. It feels like it looks crooked, like something is off or messed up, and I know people say they don’t see what I’m talking about, but I can’t really believe them.\n\nIt’s gotten to where at work, at the warehouse, I’m thinking about whether people are noticing it while I’m stocking or moving stuff around. And then if I go out, like to salsa night, it’s the same thing but worse because people are looking at you more, you know? I keep feeling like they’re seeing the left side of my face and thinking it looks strange, even if nobody says anything. Even when someone tells me, “No, you look fine,” I still feel like they’re just being nice or they don’t want to say it.\n\nSo… yeah, that’s mainly why I came in. I’m kind of stuck on it all the time.\n\nDoctor: Is it specifically the idea that the left side of your face is crooked that bothers you, or is it more about feeling like you're unattractive in general?\n\nPatient: It’s more specifically the left side of my face, yeah. I mean, I don’t feel great about how I look in general because of it, but the thing I get stuck on is that it looks crooked or messed up on that side. Like if someone is standing on my left or I’m turned a certain way, I start thinking, “They can see it, they’re noticing how off it looks.”\n\nAt the warehouse it happens a lot because I’m around people all day, moving past them, turning my head, talking while stocking stuff, and I’ll be thinking about whether they’re looking at that side of my face. And at salsa night it’s even harder because people are face-to-face with you and you’re moving close to them, so I feel like there’s no way they’re not seeing it.\n\nPeople tell me they don’t notice anything, but it doesn’t really settle it for me. I keep thinking maybe they’re just being polite or they don’t want to make me feel bad. So, yeah, it’s not just a general “I’m unattractive” thing. It’s mainly that one part of my face looks wrong to me, and then everything else kind of spirals from that.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever think that maybe your face actually doesn't look crooked, and it's just your imagination?\n\nPatient: I mean… I try to tell myself that sometimes. Like, I’ll think, “Maybe I’m making too much of it, maybe other people really don’t see it.” But it doesn’t stick. It’s like the thought comes right back that the left side of my face looks crooked or messed up.\n\nEspecially at the warehouse, when I’m around people and moving past them or talking to someone, I’ll suddenly be thinking, “They’re looking at that side, they can tell.” And then at salsa night it’s even worse because people are close up, face-to-face, and I feel like there’s no way they’re not noticing it.\n\nSo yeah, part of me hears people when they say they don’t see anything, but I can’t really believe it. I keep thinking they’re just being nice, or maybe they do notice but don’t want to say it. It feels too real to just be my imagination, even though I wish I could convince myself it was.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever thought that maybe your face could look okay, or even good, if you could just change something about the left side, like with makeup or surgery?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, that’s kind of the thing I get stuck on, I guess. I keep thinking if the left side of my face could just be… fixed somehow, or not look so crooked, then maybe I’d feel okay. Like maybe then I wouldn’t be constantly thinking about it when I’m at the warehouse, wondering if people are noticing while I’m working or walking past them.\n\nAnd at salsa night, it’s even worse, because people are right there in front of me, looking at my face, and I’m thinking, “They can see it. They can tell something is off.” So part of me does think, if that side looked different, then maybe I could relax and not feel like everyone is noticing it.\n\nBut it’s not like I have some clear plan or anything. It’s more that I can’t stop thinking about that one side looking messed up. Even when people say they don’t see anything, I still feel like they must notice it and are just not saying it.\n\nDoctor: Has your concern about the left side of your face affected your ability to do your job at the warehouse or enjoy activities like salsa night?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it has, more than I really want to admit. At the warehouse, I’ve actually been written up because I get behind. It’s not that I don’t know how to do the job, it’s more like I’ll get stuck worrying about how I’m standing or which side of my face people can see, and then I slow down or avoid going past certain people, or I’ll take longer than I should because I’m trying not to be noticed. Then the work piles up, and my supervisor sees that I’m delayed.\n\nAnd with salsa night, yeah, I’ve started skipping it. I used to like going, but now it feels like everybody is close up, looking right at me, and I can’t enjoy the dancing because I’m thinking about my face the whole time. I’ve also been avoiding soccer and family gatherings sometimes, especially if I think people might take pictures. That’s a big one. If someone pulls out a phone, I just feel like I need to get out of there.\n\nSo, yeah, it’s affecting work and the things I used to do for fun. It’s like I’m planning my whole day around not having people look at me too much.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would be satisfied with how you look if the left side of your face was 'fixed', or would you then find something else about your appearance to worry about?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I want to say I’d be satisfied, because in my head it feels like that’s the whole problem. Like, I’m here because I can’t stop thinking about the left side of my face looking crooked and messed up. That’s the thing I keep coming back to over and over.\n\nIf that part looked right, I imagine I’d be able to go to the warehouse and not constantly feel like people are noticing it when I’m walking by or talking to them. Or at salsa night, maybe I could actually just dance instead of thinking, “They’re seeing that side of my face, they can tell something’s wrong,” even when people say they don’t see anything.\n\nSo… I guess I hope fixing that would make it stop. But I don’t totally know. Right now it’s so focused on that left side that I can’t really picture anything else mattering as much. It just feels like if that one thing wasn’t there, I could finally relax.\n\nDoctor: Is it only how you look that bothers you, or are there other things about yourself that you worry about?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly how I look, yeah. I mean… I guess everybody has things they worry about, but the thing that takes over for me is my face. Specifically the left side. That’s what I can’t get away from.\n\nLike, I’m here because I can’t stop thinking it looks crooked or messed up somehow, and then I start feeling like everyone can see it. At the warehouse, even if I’m just stocking or walking past somebody, I’m thinking about what angle they’re seeing me from, whether they’re noticing that side of my face. And at salsa night it’s worse because people are right there, facing me, and I feel like they must be looking at it, even when they say they don’t see anything.\n\nSo I wouldn’t say I’m sitting around worrying about, like, my whole personality or every part of my life. It’s really this appearance thing, this one part of my face, and it just keeps looping in my head.\n\nDoctor: Has your worry about the left side of your face caused you to avoid social interactions or relationships altogether?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t think I’ve cut off everyone completely, but I definitely avoid a lot now. Like, if I know there’s going to be people around where I can’t control how they see me, or if there might be pictures, I’ll just not go.\n\nI’ve started skipping soccer, even though I used to like it, because after the game people hang around and talk, and I start thinking they’re looking at the left side of my face. Salsa nights too… I’ve skipped a bunch of those because it feels too close, like people are right in front of me and there’s nowhere to hide. And family gatherings have gotten harder, because somebody always wants to take pictures or videos, and then I’m stuck worrying about how my face looks in them.\n\nEven at work it affects me socially, kind of. At the warehouse I’ve been written up because I get delayed, and part of that is me trying to avoid walking by people or being seen from certain angles. So, yeah… I wouldn’t say I have no relationships at all, but I’m definitely pulling away from people and avoiding things so I don’t have to deal with them looking at me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think people would judge you or think less of you as a person if they saw the left side of your face?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that’s what I’m afraid of. Like, I know people say they don’t see anything, or they’ll tell me it looks normal, but it doesn’t feel normal to me. When I’m at the warehouse and someone walks past me on that side, I immediately start thinking, “They saw it. They noticed my face is crooked. Now they’re thinking something’s wrong with me.”\n\nAnd it’s not even always like they say anything. It’s more that I imagine what they’re thinking. Like maybe they think I look weird, or off, or unattractive, or like I don’t take care of myself or something. I know that probably sounds like a lot to put on one side of my face, but in the moment it feels really real. If someone looks at me for even a second too long, especially from the left, I start feeling like they’re judging me.\n\nAt salsa night it’s even worse because you’re close to people, face to face, and I can’t stop thinking they’re noticing that side. Even if they’re just focused on dancing, in my head I’m thinking, “They can tell. They’re being polite, but they see it.” So yeah, I guess I do worry they’d think less of me, not because they actually said that, but because I can’t stop thinking my face looks messed up and that everyone can see it.\n\nDoctor: Has your concern about the left side of your face caused you significant distress or problems in your daily life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… definitely. It’s not just like I look in the mirror and feel bad for a minute and then go on with my day. It actually messes with things I’m supposed to be doing.\n\nAt work, at the warehouse, I’ve been written up because I get delayed. Like, I’ll take longer than I should because I’m trying to avoid certain aisles or avoid walking past people from the left side, or I’ll kind of pause and wait until someone moves so they don’t see me from that angle. And I know from the outside it probably just looks like I’m not doing my job fast enough, but in my head I’m trying to manage how people see my face the whole time. It’s exhausting, and then I get in trouble for being behind.\n\nAnd outside of work, it’s made my life smaller, I guess. I’ve been skipping soccer, and I used to really enjoy that. But then after the game people stand around talking, and I start worrying they’re looking at me. Salsa nights too — I’ve skipped those because it feels too close, like people are right in front of my face and I can’t hide the left side. And family stuff… I’ve been avoiding some gatherings because somebody always wants pictures or videos, and then I’m stuck thinking about how bad I’m going to look or who’s going to see it later.\n\nSo yeah, it causes a lot of distress. It’s affecting work, and it’s affecting me seeing people, even people I actually want to see.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still worry about people judging you if the left side of your face looked perfectly normal?\n\nPatient: I mean… if it actually looked perfectly normal to me, then maybe I wouldn’t worry the same way. But that’s the problem — it doesn’t look normal to me. I can’t stop seeing the left side as crooked or messed up, and once I see it that way, I just assume other people can see it too.\n\nLike at the warehouse, even if nobody says anything, I’m thinking they noticed it when they walked past me. Or at salsa night, when someone is close to my face, I’m not really able to believe they don’t see what I see. Even when people tell me they don’t notice anything, it doesn’t really calm me down for long.\n\nSo… I guess if I truly believed it looked perfectly normal, maybe I wouldn’t be as scared of being judged. But I’m here because I can’t get myself to believe that. I keep thinking something is wrong with that side of my face, and then I start thinking everyone else is noticing it too.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would worry about people judging you based on other aspects of your appearance if the left side of your face looked perfectly normal to you?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the same way. I mean, I can have normal stuff where I don’t love how I look, like anyone, but this isn’t really about my whole appearance. It keeps coming back to the left side of my face.\n\nLike, if that side looked normal to me — really normal, not just people saying it’s normal — I think most of the judging fear would probably go down a lot. Because what I’m stuck on is that it looks crooked or messed up, and then I start imagining everyone else seeing that too. At the warehouse, if someone passes me on that side, that’s what I’m thinking about. At salsa night, when someone is close to me, I’m thinking they’re noticing that side of my face.\n\nSo no, I don’t think I’d just switch to worrying about, like, my hair or my clothes or something else. I’m here because I can’t stop thinking about that one thing — the left side of my face — even when people tell me they don’t see anything.\n\nDoctor: Is there anything else about your appearance, besides the left side of your face, that you worry about?\n\nPatient: Not really… I mean, I can pick at normal things sometimes, like if my hair looks bad or I’m tired or whatever, but that’s not what gets stuck in my head.\n\nIt’s really the left side of my face. That’s the thing I can’t stop thinking about. I keep feeling like it looks crooked, or uneven, or just messed up somehow, and then once I notice it, I can’t unsee it. And then I start assuming everyone else can see it too — like people at the warehouse, if they’re walking past me or standing on that side, I’m thinking, “They noticed. They’re looking at it.” Same thing at salsa night, because people are close up and facing me, and I feel like there’s nowhere to hide it.\n\nEven when someone says they don’t see anything, I don’t really believe it for very long. So no, it’s not like I’m worried about every part of how I look. It’s mostly that one thing, the left side of my face, and it just takes over.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

It’s mostly that one thing, the left side of my face, and it just takes over.\n\n[Last Question]\nIf the left side of your face looked perfectly normal to you, do you think you would then find another part of your appearance to worry about?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t think I would, honestly. I mean, I know it probably sounds like I’m just saying that because this is the thing I’m focused on right now, but it really doesn’t feel like I’m someone who’s always searching for something wrong everywhere. It’s not like I fixate on my hair one week and my body the next week and then my clothes after that.

It’s this specific thing — the left side of my face. I’m here because I can’t stop thinking it looks crooked or messed up, and then I get stuck on the idea that everyone else can see it too. Like at the warehouse, if someone walks past me on that side, I’m immediately thinking they noticed it. Or at salsa night, when someone is close to my face, I feel like they’re seeing exactly what I’m seeing, even if they don’t say anything.

So if that side actually looked perfectly normal to me — not just someone reassuring me, but if I could really see it as normal — I don’t think I’d just move on and find another flaw. I think I’d probably feel a lot more free. Because it’s not every part of my appearance that feels wrong. It’s that one side of my face that I can’t stop checking in my mind, and even when people tell me they don’t see anything, it doesn’t really stick.

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n14\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF45.2, F42.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I’m here because I just… I can’t get my mind off my face. Specifically the left side. It feels like it looks crooked, like something is off or messed up, and I know people say they don’t see what I’m talking about, but I can’t really believe them.\n\nIt’s gotten to where at work, at the warehouse, I’m thinking about whether people are noticing it while I’m stocking or moving stuff around. And then if I go out, like to salsa night, it’s the same thing but worse because people are looking at you more, you know? I keep feeling like they’re seeing the left side of my face and thinking it looks strange, even if nobody says anything. Even when someone tells me, “No, you look fine,” I still feel like they’re just being nice or they don’t want to say it.\n\nSo… yeah, that’s mainly why I came in. I’m kind of stuck on it all the time.\n\nDoctor: Is it specifically the idea that the left side of your face is crooked that bothers you, or is it more about feeling like you're unattractive in general?\n\nPatient: It’s more specifically the left side of my face, yeah. I mean, I don’t feel great about how I look in general because of it, but the thing I get stuck on is that it looks crooked or messed up on that side. Like if someone is standing on my left or I’m turned a certain way, I start thinking, “They can see it, they’re noticing how off it looks.”\n\nAt the warehouse it happens a lot because I’m around people all day, moving past them, turning my head, talking while stocking stuff, and I’ll be thinking about whether they’re looking at that side of my face. And at salsa night it’s even harder because people are face-to-face with you and you’re moving close to them, so I feel like there’s no way they’re not seeing it.\n\nPeople tell me they don’t notice anything, but it doesn’t really settle it for me. I keep thinking maybe they’re just being polite or they don’t want to make me feel bad. So, yeah, it’s not just a general “I’m unattractive” thing. It’s mainly that one part of my face looks wrong to me, and then everything else kind of spirals from that.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever think that maybe your face actually doesn't look crooked, and it's just your imagination?\n\nPatient: I mean… I try to tell myself that sometimes. Like, I’ll think, “Maybe I’m making too much of it, maybe other people really don’t see it.” But it doesn’t stick. It’s like the thought comes right back that the left side of my face looks crooked or messed up.\n\nEspecially at the warehouse, when I’m around people and moving past them or talking to someone, I’ll suddenly be thinking, “They’re looking at that side, they can tell.” And then at salsa night it’s even worse because people are close up, face-to-face, and I feel like there’s no way they’re not noticing it.\n\nSo yeah, part of me hears people when they say they don’t see anything, but I can’t really believe it. I keep thinking they’re just being nice, or maybe they do notice but don’t want to say it. It feels too real to just be my imagination, even though I wish I could convince myself it was.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever thought that maybe your face could look okay, or even good, if you could just change something about the left side, like with makeup or surgery?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, that’s kind of the thing I get stuck on, I guess. I keep thinking if the left side of my face could just be… fixed somehow, or not look so crooked, then maybe I’d feel okay. Like maybe then I wouldn’t be constantly thinking about it when I’m at the warehouse, wondering if people are noticing while I’m working or walking past them.\n\nAnd at salsa night, it’s even worse, because people are right there in front of me, looking at my face, and I’m thinking, “They can see it. They can tell something is off.” So part of me does think, if that side looked different, then maybe I could relax and not feel like everyone is noticing it.\n\nBut it’s not like I have some clear plan or anything. It’s more that I can’t stop thinking about that one side looking messed up. Even when people say they don’t see anything, I still feel like they must notice it and are just not saying it.\n\nDoctor: Has your concern about the left side of your face affected your ability to do your job at the warehouse or enjoy activities like salsa night?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it has, more than I really want to admit. At the warehouse, I’ve actually been written up because I get behind. It’s not that I don’t know how to do the job, it’s more like I’ll get stuck worrying about how I’m standing or which side of my face people can see, and then I slow down or avoid going past certain people, or I’ll take longer than I should because I’m trying not to be noticed. Then the work piles up, and my supervisor sees that I’m delayed.\n\nAnd with salsa night, yeah, I’ve started skipping it. I used to like going, but now it feels like everybody is close up, looking right at me, and I can’t enjoy the dancing because I’m thinking about my face the whole time. I’ve also been avoiding soccer and family gatherings sometimes, especially if I think people might take pictures. That’s a big one. If someone pulls out a phone, I just feel like I need to get out of there.\n\nSo, yeah, it’s affecting work and the things I used to do for fun. It’s like I’m planning my whole day around not having people look at me too much.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would be satisfied with how you look if the left side of your face was 'fixed', or would you then find something else about your appearance to worry about?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I want to say I’d be satisfied, because in my head it feels like that’s the whole problem. Like, I’m here because I can’t stop thinking about the left side of my face looking crooked and messed up. That’s the thing I keep coming back to over and over.\n\nIf that part looked right, I imagine I’d be able to go to the warehouse and not constantly feel like people are noticing it when I’m walking by or talking to them. Or at salsa night, maybe I could actually just dance instead of thinking, “They’re seeing that side of my face, they can tell something’s wrong,” even when people say they don’t see anything.\n\nSo… I guess I hope fixing that would make it stop. But I don’t totally know. Right now it’s so focused on that left side that I can’t really picture anything else mattering as much. It just feels like if that one thing wasn’t there, I could finally relax.\n\nDoctor: Is it only how you look that bothers you, or are there other things about yourself that you worry about?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly how I look, yeah. I mean… I guess everybody has things they worry about, but the thing that takes over for me is my face. Specifically the left side. That’s what I can’t get away from.\n\nLike, I’m here because I can’t stop thinking it looks crooked or messed up somehow, and then I start feeling like everyone can see it. At the warehouse, even if I’m just stocking or walking past somebody, I’m thinking about what angle they’re seeing me from, whether they’re noticing that side of my face. And at salsa night it’s worse because people are right there, facing me, and I feel like they must be looking at it, even when they say they don’t see anything.\n\nSo I wouldn’t say I’m sitting around worrying about, like, my whole personality or every part of my life. It’s really this appearance thing, this one part of my face, and it just keeps looping in my head.\n\nDoctor: Has your worry about the left side of your face caused you to avoid social interactions or relationships altogether?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t think I’ve cut off everyone completely, but I definitely avoid a lot now. Like, if I know there’s going to be people around where I can’t control how they see me, or if there might be pictures, I’ll just not go.\n\nI’ve started skipping soccer, even though I used to like it, because after the game people hang around and talk, and I start thinking they’re looking at the left side of my face. Salsa nights too… I’ve skipped a bunch of those because it feels too close, like people are right in front of me and there’s nowhere to hide. And family gatherings have gotten harder, because somebody always wants to take pictures or videos, and then I’m stuck worrying about how my face looks in them.\n\nEven at work it affects me socially, kind of. At the warehouse I’ve been written up because I get delayed, and part of that is me trying to avoid walking by people or being seen from certain angles. So, yeah… I wouldn’t say I have no relationships at all, but I’m definitely pulling away from people and avoiding things so I don’t have to deal with them looking at me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think people would judge you or think less of you as a person if they saw the left side of your face?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that’s what I’m afraid of. Like, I know people say they don’t see anything, or they’ll tell me it looks normal, but it doesn’t feel normal to me. When I’m at the warehouse and someone walks past me on that side, I immediately start thinking, “They saw it. They noticed my face is crooked. Now they’re thinking something’s wrong with me.”\n\nAnd it’s not even always like they say anything. It’s more that I imagine what they’re thinking. Like maybe they think I look weird, or off, or unattractive, or like I don’t take care of myself or something. I know that probably sounds like a lot to put on one side of my face, but in the moment it feels really real. If someone looks at me for even a second too long, especially from the left, I start feeling like they’re judging me.\n\nAt salsa night it’s even worse because you’re close to people, face to face, and I can’t stop thinking they’re noticing that side. Even if they’re just focused on dancing, in my head I’m thinking, “They can tell. They’re being polite, but they see it.” So yeah, I guess I do worry they’d think less of me, not because they actually said that, but because I can’t stop thinking my face looks messed up and that everyone can see it.\n\nDoctor: Has your concern about the left side of your face caused you significant distress or problems in your daily life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… definitely. It’s not just like I look in the mirror and feel bad for a minute and then go on with my day. It actually messes with things I’m supposed to be doing.\n\nAt work, at the warehouse, I’ve been written up because I get delayed. Like, I’ll take longer than I should because I’m trying to avoid certain aisles or avoid walking past people from the left side, or I’ll kind of pause and wait until someone moves so they don’t see me from that angle. And I know from the outside it probably just looks like I’m not doing my job fast enough, but in my head I’m trying to manage how people see my face the whole time. It’s exhausting, and then I get in trouble for being behind.\n\nAnd outside of work, it’s made my life smaller, I guess. I’ve been skipping soccer, and I used to really enjoy that. But then after the game people stand around talking, and I start worrying they’re looking at me. Salsa nights too — I’ve skipped those because it feels too close, like people are right in front of my face and I can’t hide the left side. And family stuff… I’ve been avoiding some gatherings because somebody always wants pictures or videos, and then I’m stuck thinking about how bad I’m going to look or who’s going to see it later.\n\nSo yeah, it causes a lot of distress. It’s affecting work, and it’s affecting me seeing people, even people I actually want to see.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still worry about people judging you if the left side of your face looked perfectly normal?\n\nPatient: I mean… if it actually looked perfectly normal to me, then maybe I wouldn’t worry the same way. But that’s the problem — it doesn’t look normal to me. I can’t stop seeing the left side as crooked or messed up, and once I see it that way, I just assume other people can see it too.\n\nLike at the warehouse, even if nobody says anything, I’m thinking they noticed it when they walked past me. Or at salsa night, when someone is close to my face, I’m not really able to believe they don’t see what I see. Even when people tell me they don’t notice anything, it doesn’t really calm me down for long.\n\nSo… I guess if I truly believed it looked perfectly normal, maybe I wouldn’t be as scared of being judged. But I’m here because I can’t get myself to believe that. I keep thinking something is wrong with that side of my face, and then I start thinking everyone else is noticing it too.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would worry about people judging you based on other aspects of your appearance if the left side of your face looked perfectly normal to you?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the same way. I mean, I can have normal stuff where I don’t love how I look, like anyone, but this isn’t really about my whole appearance. It keeps coming back to the left side of my face.\n\nLike, if that side looked normal to me — really normal, not just people saying it’s normal — I think most of the judging fear would probably go down a lot.
