Because the whole thing I’m here for is that I can’t stop thinking the left side of my face looks crooked or messed up, and once that thought gets in my head, it’s like everything else starts getting filtered through it.\n\nLike at work, at the warehouse, if someone walks past me on that side or talks to me from that side, I’m not just having a normal conversation. I’m thinking, “They can see it. They noticed something’s wrong.” And then at salsa night it’s even worse sometimes because people are close to my face and looking right at me, and I’m trying to act normal, but in my head I’m stuck on whether they’re noticing that side of my face.\n\nSo if I could actually look at it and see it as normal — not just have someone tell me it’s fine, because people do say that and it doesn’t really stick — but if I believed it myself, then I think the concern would pretty much be gone. I don’t think I’d be sitting here the same way. It’s that left side of my face that keeps taking over.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe that if the left side of your face looked perfectly normal to you, you would then find another aspect of your appearance to be overly concerned about?\n\nPatient: I don’t really believe that, no. I mean, I understand why you’re asking, because maybe it could seem like if it wasn’t my face then I’d just move the worry somewhere else, but that’s not how it feels to me.\n\nI’m here because I can’t stop thinking that the left side of my face looks crooked and messed up. That’s the thing I keep coming back to. It’s not like I’m scanning my whole body all day looking for a new problem. It’s more like this one specific part gets stuck in my head and then everything kind of revolves around it.\n\nLike at the warehouse, if someone is near me or walks past on my left side, I immediately start thinking they’re noticing it. Even if they don’t say anything, even if they’re just doing their job, my mind goes straight to, “They saw it.” And at salsa night, when people are close to my face, it’s really hard not to feel like they can see exactly what I’m seeing. People can tell me they don’t notice anything, but it doesn’t really make it go away for me.\n\nSo if the left side of my face actually looked normal to me — like I truly believed it looked normal — I don’t think I’d suddenly pick another feature to be overly concerned about. I think I’d just feel relieved, honestly. It’s this one thing that keeps taking over.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your perception of your face being crooked is based on something that you see, or is it more of a feeling that you have?\n\nPatient: It feels like both, honestly, but it starts with what I think I’m seeing. Like, I’ll look at my face and the left side just looks wrong to me — crooked, uneven, kind of messed up. And once I see it that way, then the feeling takes over too, like this strong feeling that other people must be seeing the same thing.\n\nSo at the warehouse, if someone is standing on that side of me or walking past me, I’m not just thinking about work anymore. I’m thinking, “They’re looking at it. They notice it.” And at salsa night, because people are closer and facing me, it gets even harder to shake. Even when someone says they don’t see anything, it doesn’t really cancel out what I’m seeing or what I feel like they’re noticing.\n\nSo I guess I’d say it’s based on something I see in the mirror or in my own face, but then it becomes this feeling I can’t get away from. Like I know people tell me it’s not there, but to me it looks there.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your perception of your face being crooked might be something that only you can see, and not something that is actually there?\n\nPatient: I mean… that’s the part I keep going back and forth on. People tell me they don’t see anything, or they act like they don’t know what I’m talking about, and I try to take that in. I really do. But when I look at myself, especially that left side of my face, it looks crooked and messed up to me. It doesn’t feel like I’m making it up on purpose or just imagining it out of nowhere.\n\nBut then, yeah, sometimes I wonder if maybe it’s something only I can see, because if it was as obvious as it feels to me, wouldn’t someone at the warehouse have said something? Or at salsa night, when people are close to me and looking right at me, wouldn’t they react more? That’s what confuses me. They’ll say they don’t notice anything, but I’m still sitting there thinking, “No, they must be noticing it and just not saying it.”\n\nSo I guess I can admit it might be something only I’m seeing, or at least that I’m seeing way more strongly than other people are. But it’s hard for me to fully believe it’s not actually there, because in my head it feels so real. I’m here because I can’t stop thinking about it — that the left side of my face looks wrong — and I keep feeling like everyone at work or at salsa is noticing it even when they tell me they don’t see anything.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out.
